So `uses:` is pinned to the CLI release tag `v#{Cybertrain::VERSION}` (the same tag as the dependency `cybertrain new` writes into spin.toml), and that only decides the action's steps. The CLI that installs Spinel is the one from the commit the action read from spin.lock, so it is always the same version as the framework the application compiles. When an application upgrades the framework, it does not need to bump the action's tag.
  - The framework is fetched with git into `$RUNNER_TEMP`, not with `actions/checkout` into `vendor/` in the working tree. `actions/checkout` cannot place it outside the working tree, and inside the working tree it would be seen by spin (which has features that use `vendor/`) and by the application's `git diff`.
  - The cache key is not a hash of `version.rb` but the `SPINEL_TAG` read from it: `version.rb` changes with every release, but the Spinel build is the same as long as `SPINEL_TAG` is the same. `hashFiles` may also be unable to read files outside the working tree.
  - An application with a path dependency (`cybertrain new --path`) has no commit in spin.lock, so the action fails with the reason. If you check out the framework yourself and pass `framework-path`, it uses that without reading spin.lock.
- Rejected alternatives:
  - Writing out all the steps in the workflow: the source of truth for the steps would live in generated code, and changes would not reach existing applications (the very problem in the issue).
  - Choosing the framework commit inside the action as the equivalent of `uses:` (a dynamic `uses:`): GitHub Actions does not have it.
  - Replacing the hand-written servers in test/http_client.rb and test/mailer.rb with the receivers: those exist to play abnormal cases step by step, such as the intervals between split byte sends, malformed responses, cutting off midway and plain text after STARTTLS, and they do not fit the shape of the receivers (a well-behaved peer). They were left untouched so their output and determinism do not change, and the receiver tests were written new in `test/receivers.rb`.
- Deferred: a TLS receiver (certificates cannot be created, D24), multiple SMTP messages in one connection (all but the last remain only in `transcript`), and keep-alive on one receiver connection. The action tag `v<VERSION>` can be used from the release that contains the action (the next release). Earlier tags (up to v0.2.1) do not have the action.
- Verification: `test/receivers.rb` (HttpClient with GET / POST / chunked response / 204; chunked request bodies and duplicate headers over a raw socket; `max` and `respond_with`; a request that never arrives giving nil at the limit, and the next receiver receiving on the same port after `stop`; idempotence of `stop`; the port closing with `accept_window`; Mailer with the transcript, envelope and message bytes; with and without 8BITMIME; rejection at RCPT, greeting and after the body; AUTH PLAIN; two messages with `Mailer.deliver`; a message that never arrives) gives the same output under CRuby and after compilation. A version with openssl added, and a version with the receivers added to the whole framework (the same requires as test/template_integration.rb, including Server and Test::Client), were also compiled temporarily and confirmed to pass. For CI, `test/cli_new.rb` checks the contents of the workflow, and loading the YAML and `spin run gen -- --check` were checked on an application created with `cybertrain new`. The action's YAML was also loaded, and the Ruby one-liner that reads spin.lock was checked with spin.lock files for a git dependency and a path dependency.

### D33. Outbound WebSocket client and long-lived subscriptions: a synchronous client on top of `Net`, started together with the server via `Background` (2026-10-26)

- Context: There was no way to stay connected to an external data feed (market ticks, telemetry, event streams) for as long as the server runs and hand the arriving messages to the application. Spinel's `Net::HTTP` equivalent cannot be used for the same reason as in D24: when the wait is inside C, one stalled peer holds an OS worker (with the default `SPINEL_WORKERS=1`, the whole server). There is no WebSocket standard library either.
- Decision: Two layers (the same shape as the relationship between `HttpClient` and `Scheduler`).
  - `cybertrain/websocket/frame.rb`: RFC 6455 frame encoding and decoding, and the Accept key (`Digest::SHA1`). Pure functions that never touch a socket; they run on CRuby too. The error hierarchy lives here as well: under `WebSocket::Error`, `TimeoutError` / `RefusedError` / `TLSError` / `BlockedAddressError` / `HandshakeError` / `ProtocolError` / `TooLargeError` / `ClosedError` (`close_code`, `close_reason`).
  - `cybertrain/websocket/client.rb`: the synchronous client `WebSocket::Client` (`connect` / `send_text` / `send_binary` / `send_ping` / `receive(wait)` / `close`). URL parsing, rejection of private addresses and trying every address are shared with `HttpClient` (`parse_url_for`, `dial`) rather than duplicated. Waits use `Net::Timer` (`timeout` per operation, `timeout * 3` overall); a `receive(wait)` timeout returns `nil` rather than raising. If the timeout hits in the middle of a frame, the data stays in the `Reader` buffer and the next `receive` continues from there. Sends are always masked and go out as a single frame.
  - `cybertrain/websocket.rb`: `WebSocket.subscribe(name, url, ...)` registers a `Subscription` (`on_open` / `on_message`, both return `self`). One thread per subscription (started via a method: NOTES rule 21). It repeats `receive(1)` and checks for a stop request and the clock every second. After `ping_interval` (15 seconds) of quiet it sends a ping; after `stale_after` (45 seconds) of silence it closes and reconnects. The reconnect wait is `min(60, 2 ** (attempt - 1))` seconds ±20 %, and returns to 1 second after a connection that lasted 60 seconds. An exception in `on_message` is printed as `[ERROR]` and the connection is kept; an exception in `on_open` closes the connection and reconnects. Waits are `wait_readable` or `sleep`, so a stalled feed does not hold an OS worker while it waits. However, each connection attempt looks up the host name with blocking `getaddrinfo` (`Client.connect` -> `HttpClient.dial` -> `Net.resolve`, D24) and holds the worker during that. With `SPINEL_WORKERS=1` and a dead resolver, requests stall for the resolver timeout for each feed and each attempt (README "Outbound HTTP", "Name resolution blocks"). SQLite inside `on_message` also holds the worker, just as in `Scheduler` tasks. `stop!` cuts off the joins of all threads at 2 seconds in total, and a connected feed sends a close and waits at most 1 second for the server's close. When it returns, `connected?` is false for every feed.
  - `cybertrain/background.rb`: a registry of `Background::Service` (`service_start(config)` / `service_stop`), whose default does nothing. `Application#serve` calls `Background.start_all` after `Scheduler.start!`, and `Background.stop_all` on shutdown. `cybertrain/websocket.rb` registers itself when it is required. **`application.rb` does not require `cybertrain/websocket`**: to keep libssl out of applications that do not use it, this follows the same substitution pattern as `Net.tls_connector`: "the default does nothing, and only the programs that need the real one require it". It does not start with `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0` (`config.worker = false`) (`Background.start_all` returns 0 first, so the only notice is `Scheduler`'s "run elsewhere" line; when `WebSocket.start!` is called directly it prints its own line), and `migrate` / `db ...` / `Main.command` / `Test::Client` do not start it.
  - `cybertrain/test/websocket_receiver.rb`: a scripted WebSocket server in the style of the D32 receivers (bounded accept and reads, threads started via a method). A sequence of `WsStep`s plays out the handshake response, frames to send, frames to expect, silence and disconnects, and records the frames the client sent, unmasked.
- Judgement calls (decided during implementation):
  - Status is read with `WebSocket.feed_status(name)` (`connected?`, `last_message_at`, `reconnects`). The name `status` was avoided because a `status` with a different return type exists elsewhere in the framework (`DB::CLI.status`, `Migrator#status`) and would break Spinel's whole-program inference (NOTES rules 34 / 41). The three values are read separately (they are not one atomic snapshot). It is not built into `/up`.
  - Loss of the connection or a close from the peer after the connection is established is `ClosedError` (a reset is 1006; write timeouts and TLS failures stay `TimeoutError` / `TLSError`). Failures while connecting are `RefusedError` / `TimeoutError` / `TLSError` / `BlockedAddressError` / `HandshakeError`.
  - `connected?` becomes true at the moment the connection is made, before `on_open` runs.
  - `stop!` `join`s each subscription's thread for at most 2 seconds (a generation number keeps an old loop from running alongside a new `start!`).
  - `test/websocket_integration.rb` compiles the whole framework together with the WebSocket code. This is because `test/websocket_client.rb` and `test/websocket_subscription.rb` require only the WebSocket files, so a name clash with another file (like `status` above) is not found there and first breaks in the application binary (NOTES rule 41).
- Rejected:
  - `application.rb` calls `WebSocket.start!` directly: brings libssl into every application.
  - The application calls `start!` from `config/app.rb`: the user would need to know the startup order (database connection, port binding, `SPINEL_WORKERS`). `Background` makes the framework own that order.
  - Extensions (`permessage-deflate`) and subprotocols: not offered in the request; if the server returns one, the handshake fails.
  - Proxies, redirects, cookies, sending fragmented messages.
  - Automatically storing received messages or enqueueing them as a `Job`: if needed, the application does it inside `on_message`.
- Deferred: adding and removing subscriptions while running (`subscribe` is called before the server starts), sending from outside a subscription (`Connection` is valid only inside callbacks), UTF-8 validation of text frames (passed on as bytes; a deviation from RFC 6455 8.1), end-to-end tests of `wss` (as in D24, certificates cannot be created). (The WebSocket server arrived as D36; feed timers, the restart of a dead feed thread and the feed health check as D56.)
- Consequences: Subscriptions are written in `config/app.rb` (or a file required before `Main.run`). SQLite calls in `on_message` hold the OS worker until they return, so a high-frequency feed should use a light INSERT, or buffer in memory and write in batches from a `Scheduler` task. If several processes have the same subscription, they receive the same feed twice, so, as with `Scheduler`, turn it off with `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0`.
- Revised 2026-11-05 (D39): the feeds can run in a process of their own. `./<app> feeds` starts every subscription, and `./<app> feeds NAME...` only the named ones (an unknown name is a boot error listing the registered ones), with no port, no jobs and no scheduler, so a deployment runs one container per feed as fin_dash does; the web role then sets `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0`. `WebSocket.start!(config, only: names)` takes the list, and `Background::Service` gained `service_start_only(config, names)` (the default starts the service only when no name is given: a named feed process starts no other service) and `service_feed_names`. A feed callback can broadcast to browsers with `Stream.broadcast` (D37), which is how a feed reaches a page. The server side, "deferred" above, arrived as D36.
- Revised 2026-11-09 (phase 3, D56): `on_tick` runs work on the feed's own thread; a supervisor restarts a feed thread that ended and a `feeds` process exits with status 1 after repeated deaths; `feed_status` gains `restarts` and `thread_alive?`; the status is published in `cybertrain_feeds`, and `GET /up` reports a stale feed (the judgement call "it is not built into `/up`" above is superseded).

### D30. Operations: `db backup` (VACUUM INTO), `db vacuum`, and a `GET /up` health check (2026-10-25, issue #76)

- Context: A deployed application's data is a single SQLite file, and every application was rebuilding the same three things: a consistent backup taken without stopping the running server, the work of actually shrinking the file after deletes, and a health check that reaches the database (`public/up.html` is a static file and shows only that the process answers HTTP).
- Decision:
  - Add `backup` and `vacuum` to `Cybertrain::DB::CLI` (`./<app> db backup ...`, or `bin/db.rb` in development). The target is the configured database (`CYBERTRAIN_DATABASE`). `:memory:` and `file:` URIs are rejected (there is no file to copy, or no plain path to compare with the target). `Main.run` passes the application name to `DB::CLI.run(argv, root, name)` (used for dated file names; it is empty from `bin/db.rb`, in which case the base of the database file name is used).
  - `db backup PATH [--force]`: `VACUUM INTO ?` on a dedicated connection (the file name is a bound parameter, outside any transaction. SQLite 3.27 or later is checked with `sqlite_version()`; if older, it prints the reason and exits with 1). It writes to a new name inside a temporary directory `PATH.tmp-<pid>-<random>/`, created in the same directory as PATH with `Dir.mkdir(..., 0o700)` + `chmod 0700`, and lets VACUUM INTO create the file itself (SQLite on Linux accepts a pre-created empty file, but SQLite on macOS rejects it with "output file already exists". Because the directory is 0700, other users cannot open the file, whatever its mode right after creation). On completion it does `chmod 0600`, `fsync`, and `File.rename` onto PATH, and removes the temporary directory. On failure it removes the temporary file (and companions such as `-journal`) and re-raises. The rescue sits in an ordinary method that does not yield (NOTES 32 / 45). An existing PATH is rejected without `--force`, the database itself is rejected even with `--force`, and a missing parent directory is rejected (checked before opening, so that no message contains the temporary file name).
  - `db backup DIR/ [--keep N]`: if the argument ends in `/` or is an existing directory, it writes to `DIR/<app>-<YYYYmmddTHHMMSSZ>.sqlite3` (UTC). A missing directory is created with 0700. `--keep N` counts only names that exactly match that form (`dated_backup?`; the timestamps sort in string order) and removes the oldest ones beyond N. It does not touch other applications' names, temporary files, or files the operator put there. A second backup within the same second is rejected as existing.
  - `db vacuum`: `VACUUM` followed by `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)`. In WAL mode the result of VACUUM is first written to the WAL, so disk space is freed only once the checkpoint copies it back into the database file, truncates the file to its new size and empties the WAL. It prints the sizes before and after (main file + WAL). If a reader keeps the checkpoint from finishing (busy), it says so.
  - `Config#health_path` (default `"/up"`, `CYBERTRAIN_HEALTH_PATH`; empty disables it; boot stops if it does not start with `/`) and `Config#health_worker_stale` (default 300 seconds; boot stops if it is below 1). The `Cybertrain::HealthCheck` middleware sits inside `RequestLogger` and outside `Static`. It answers only GET / HEAD for exactly that path, and passes other methods and paths down. Because it comes before sessions, CSRF and the router, no controller is needed, no login `before_action` runs, and no cookie is set. Logging is just the usual two lines of a request.
  - Check: `DB.with { |c| c.execute("SELECT count(*) AS n FROM sqlite_master") }`. A bare `SELECT 1` does not read the file, so the query reads the schema page (page 1). `sqlite_schema` is an alias only from 3.33, so it is written as `sqlite_master`. On `Cybertrain::DB::Error` (and StandardError), `503 database unavailable`. If the database answers, and `Scheduler.running?` and `Scheduler.last_tick` is older than `health_worker_stale` seconds, `503 worker stale` (the 0 before the first tick is not considered stale). Otherwise `200 ok`. All of them with `text/plain; charset=utf-8` and `Cache-Control: no-store`.
- Reasons:
  - **Temporary file and rename.** If VACUUM INTO fails partway, a half-written file is left under the real name. Finding out that it is broken only at restore time is the worst outcome, so only a finished file ever appears under the real name. The contents are password digests and session tokens, so the file is 0600 from the moment it is created (creating it under the umask and then running chmod leaves a window in which other users can open it).
  - **From the CLI, in a separate process.** An SQLite call holds the OS worker until it returns (the D28 constraint). Running VACUUM INTO on a large database on the scheduler thread stops every request with `SPINEL_WORKERS=1`. The README describes running the CLI from cron / a systemd timer, or running it as a child process with `system("./<app>", "db", "backup", ...)` from a task the server itself schedules. It was confirmed that `system` blocks only the calling thread (NOTES 75; `spawn` does not compile, and `Process.spawn` does but there is no `Process.wait`).
  - **A built-in handler before the router.** Written as a route and a controller, in an application with authentication the `before_action` of `ApplicationController` would redirect the health check to the sign-in page, and a session cookie would be set too. Rails avoids this by putting `rails/health#show` directly under `ActionController::Base`, but here not going through the route table at all is simpler.
  - **worker stale.** A stuck task (an infinite loop, an external call that never returns) stops jobs while keeping the process alive. As long as the process answers HTTP this is invisible from outside, so the check looks at `last_tick` (provided in D28). Processes that do not run the scheduler (`CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0`, applications without tasks) are not checked.
- Rejected:
  - Copying with the `sqlite3_backup_*` API: it can copy incrementally, but it adds FFI functions and may restart from the beginning on every write. VACUUM INTO is a single SQL statement and produces a compact copy without free pages.
  - Building backups into the server as a scheduler task: the OS worker problem above.
  - Keeping `public/up.html` and making do with it as the health check: it returns 200 even without a database.
  - Changing the Kamal configuration that `cybertrain new` writes (`healthcheck: path: /up.html` in `config/deploy.yml`) to `/up`: this spans the generated files and their CI and tests (`test/cli_new.rb`, `examples/blog`, the workflow), and touches the same files as the parallel work on the `cybertrain new` CI template (#78), so this change only recommends switching to `/up` in the documentation.
- Consequences: Because `HealthCheck` is in the default stack, the expected stack order in `test/application.rb` and `test/multipart_app.rb` changed. `backup` / `vacuum` were added to `Main.usage` and `DB::CLI.usage` (snapshots such as `test/main.rb`). The CRuby-side `cybertrain db ...` passes any arguments unchanged to `spin run db -- ...`, so it was not changed (nor was its usage string, because that goes into what `cybertrain new` generates).
- Verification: `test/db_cli_backup.rb` (the 0600 copy opens with every row, no temporary files are left, overwrite rejection and `--force`, rejection of the database itself, a failed copy from a corrupt file leaves nothing behind, rejection of a missing directory, a missing database and `:memory:`, argument errors, the form of dated names, `--keep` removes only its own form, the 0700 directory, rejection within the same second, vacuum after deletes shrinks the file and empties the WAL) and `test/health.rb` (position in the stack, 200 / no-store / no cookie / before_action does not run, HEAD and query strings, other paths and methods, two log lines, 503 after the pool is closed or disconnected, disabling, the environment variable, the boot checks, 200 even when stale while the scheduler is stopped, `worker stale` when a real thread is started and `last_tick` is made stale).

### D34. CI cost: run one Ubuntu job on pull requests, stop re-running on main what the pull request already tested, and test locally first (proposal, 2026-10-25; only the test shards implemented, see the status at the end)

- Context: every push to a pull request that touches `cybertrain/**` (15 of the last 20 pull requests) starts five workflows, and the merge to main runs them all again on what is almost always the same tree. The repository is public, so standard runners cost nothing, but the minutes count in the Actions usage metrics and use the concurrency limits (20 jobs on Free, 40 on Pro, 5 macOS jobs on either); were the repository made private, macOS would bill at 10x (15 minutes = 150). Measured over the last 60 runs, 2026-10-23 to 2026-10-25 (runner minutes):

| Workflow | Trigger | Per run | Main content |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CI / test (ubuntu-latest) | PR and main push | 21 min | `spin test` 9 min (82 test programs, 4 vCPU), E2E (shop 5, album 4, gitshop 1), examples/blog 1.5 |
| CI / test (macos-latest) | same | 15 min | the same steps; arm64 is faster (`spin test` 6 min) |
| Playground image | same (`cybertrain/**` and others) | 7-13 min | docker build 5.5 (gha cache), smoke.sh 5.6 |
| Blog image | same | 4-5 min | docker build 4 (rebuilds Spinel every time, see below) |
| Gate | every PR and main push | 0.2-1 min | `script/gate` |
| Pages | main push only | 0.5 min | YARD and deploy |

  - Per pull request: one push costs about 51 minutes (21 + 15 + 10 + 5 + 0.3), the merge about 52 (the same plus Pages), so two pushes and a merge cost about 155. 68 merges in the last 30 days, 40 in the last 7; at 6 a day that is about 930 minutes a day, about 28,000 a month. On 2026-10-25 at 10:22 six merges within one minute started 30 runs (concurrency cancelled 20, but their start and checkout were already spent).
  - The Spinel toolchain cache works (restore 1 s, `cybertrain setup` 1 s). The slow part is compiling the framework and the tests: `spin test` builds `build/` from scratch on every run.
  - Locally (a Claude cloud session, 4 vCPU, 16 GB): first `spin test` 14 min 02 s; a second run with nothing changed 11 s (0 recompiled); touching `cybertrain/version.rb` (same content) 13 min 59 s; touching only `test/router.rb` 13 min 10 s (82 recompiled either way); `spin test test/<name>.rb` recompiles one program, a few seconds to a few tens of seconds. spin's freshness check is "is any input of the package newer than the output", not a dependency graph, so a kept `build/` helps only when nothing changed.
  - The one CI failure in those 60 runs (2026-10-23 18:25, PR #61) failed on macOS only, in `test/cli_toolchain.rb` ("the build lock is a directory ... taken atomically", a file-system difference); Ubuntu passed. OS differences are real and cluster around the CLI toolchain (`cybertrain/cli/toolchain.rb`, processes and file locks, OpenSSL linking).
- Decision (proposed): split what runs on a pull request from what runs on main, skip the main re-run when it would test the same tree, and make one local command the source of the CI steps.

| Job | Pull request | Main push |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Gate | yes | yes |
| CI ubuntu | yes, unless docs-only | yes, unless the merge tree equals the PR head |
| CI macos | only when the change matches `MACOS_PATHS`, or with label `ci:macos` | yes (or once a day, see below) |
| Playground image | only for `playground/`, `.devcontainer/`, the Dockerfile, its workflow | yes (it publishes `:latest`) |
| Blog image | only for `examples/blog/`, the Dockerfile, its workflow | yes |
| Pages | - | yes, unchanged |

  - A first job `plan` (about 10 s, `actions/checkout` with `fetch-depth: 0`) outputs the matrix `os` as JSON. On a pull request it takes `git diff --name-only "origin/$GITHUB_BASE_REF...HEAD"`: if every changed file matches `DOCS_ONLY` (`^(site/|.*\.md$|script/|\.githooks/)`, the rule now in the comment and `paths` at the top of ci.yml) and none matches `TESTED_ANYWAY` (`^(docs/api/|script/api-docs$)`, the API reference inputs), ubuntu is dropped; macOS is added when a file matches `MACOS_PATHS='^(cybertrain/cli/|cybertrain/cli\.rb|cybertrain/shell\.rb|cybertrain/server|cybertrain/dev/|cybertrain/version\.rb|exe/|test/cli_|test/server|test/dev_|\.github/workflows/ci\.yml|spin\.toml)'` (`version.rb` for Spinel release bumps; together with `cybertrain/cli/` about 20% of pull requests, so macOS runs on pull requests drop to about 1/5); the label `ci:macos` (add `labeled` to `pull_request.types`) runs both OSes and overrides the docs-only check. On a push to main macOS is always in the matrix, and ubuntu is dropped when HEAD is a merge commit (`git rev-list --parents -n1 HEAD` gives 3 words) whose tree equals its second parent's (`HEAD^{tree}` = `HEAD^2^{tree}`), which holds when main did not move between the branch point and the merge (most recent merges).
  - The skip removes ubuntu from the matrix rather than using a job-level `if:`, which would stop the whole matrix, macOS included. `test` has `needs: plan`, `if: needs.plan.outputs.os != '[]'`, `fail-fast: false`, `matrix.os: ${{ fromJSON(needs.plan.outputs.os) }}`.
  - `on.pull_request.paths` is removed from ci.yml and its rule moves into `plan`: a workflow skipped by `paths` creates no check and stays Pending, so a required check would block the merge, while a job skipped by `if:` reports Success. The required check is a final job `ci` (`needs: [plan, test]`, `if: always()`), failing in an expression (needs values are not expanded into the shell) when `needs.plan.result != 'success'`, or `needs.test.result != 'success'` unless test was skipped with `os == '[]'`. `test` itself is not required, since a failed `plan` skips it and a skip reports Success. Branch protection requires `Gate` and `ci`.
  - Optional schedule variant: add `on.schedule: cron "0 3 * * *"`; on `schedule` the matrix is macOS only (`ubuntu=0; macos=1`) on the tip of main, and on `push` `macos=0`. Same `test` job, no separate workflow.
  - Playground and Blog image: remove `cybertrain/**`, `cybertrain.rb`, `cybertrain.gemspec`, `spin.toml` and `exe/**` from `pull_request.paths`, keep them in `push.paths`. Optionally a `ci:images` label opts a pull request in, the same way as `ci:macos`.
  - Shorter ubuntu job (21 -> 17 min, macOS 15 -> 12): merge the album E2E into shop (`cybertrain generate scaffold post title:string cover:attachment` in the same app, one build); gitshop stays (only `spin run gen -- --check` and migrate).
  - Blog Dockerfile layers: copy only the CLI files the gemspec lists (`cybertrain.gemspec cybertrain.rb spin.toml README.md`, `exe/`, `cybertrain/version.rb app_name.rb shell.rb ident.rb cli.rb`, `cybertrain/cli/`, `cybertrain/generator/inflector.rb`), run `ruby -I/src exe/cybertrain setup`, then `COPY cybertrain/` and `COPY examples/blog/`, as playground/Dockerfile already does; a framework change then no longer rebuilds Spinel (3 min). Blog image 4 -> 1.5 min. Extend the `script/gate` check "playground gem stage copies spec.files" to this Dockerfile's pre-setup COPY lines (spec.files is the CLI only, 15 files).
  - `script/ci-local` (new): the CI ubuntu job's steps in one command. `script/ci-local` runs gate, `spin test`, examples/blog's gen freshness and `spin test`, api-docs; `--e2e` adds the CI E2E (`cybertrain new`, scaffold, build, curl) under /tmp; `--quick` runs gate and the related tests only: changed `test/*.rb`, and for a changed `cybertrain/foo.rb` `test/foo*.rb` plus the tests `grep -l "cybertrain/foo"` finds, from `git diff --name-only main...HEAD`, falling back to all. `--quick` always names files, since one touch makes the full run a 14-minute build. The shell in ci.yml moves into the script and CI calls `script/ci-local --e2e`: the point is one place for the CI and local steps (the E2E curl sequence exists only in the yml today), not the minutes.
  - Pre-push hook `.githooks/pre-push` (installed by `script/install-hooks`) runs `script/ci-local --quick`; it takes minutes, not the pre-commit gate's 1 s, so CONTRIBUTING.md documents skipping it with `git push --no-verify` or `CYBERTRAIN_SKIP_PREPUSH=1`.
  - A root `CLAUDE.md` for Claude cloud sessions (most pull requests here are made by Claude; `script/claude-cloud-setup` already installs Spinel): run `script/ci-local` before pushing (`--e2e` when the change reaches the CLI, the generator or the templates); do not push failing, or if you must, name the failing tests and the reason in the pull request; paste the diff of regenerated `test/*.rb.expected` files into the pull request. Codespaces (`.devcontainer`) use the playground image and are out of scope.
  - PR template: "`spin test` passes" becomes "`script/ci-local` passes (paste the last line of its output)".
  - `timeout-minutes: 30` on the CI job (today only Playground 45 and Blog 30 have one), so a hung `spin test` does not run for 6 hours.
  - Unchanged: `concurrency` (`cancel-in-progress` on pull requests; main is not cancelled and pending runs fold into one, costing 0.2 min each), Pages, Gate.
- Estimate (two pushes and a merge, a `cybertrain/**` change, main not moved):

| Step | PR push x2 | Main push | Total | vs now |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Now | 51 x 2 = 102 | 52 | 155 | 100% |
| PR runs ubuntu + Gate | 21.3 x 2 = 43 | 52 | 95 | 61% |
| + skip CI ubuntu on main, keep macOS | 43 | 15 + 10 + 5 + 0.3 + 0.5 = 31 | 74 | 48% |
| + E2E merge (ubuntu 17, macOS 12) | 17.3 x 2 = 35 | 12 + 10 + 5 + 0.3 + 0.5 = 28 | 63 | 41% |
| + macOS once a day on schedule | 35 | 10 + 5 + 0.3 + 0.5 = 16 | 51 | 33% (plus 12 min a day) |

  - Per push to a pull request: about 51 -> 21 minutes. Per month (6 merges a day x 30 = 180 pull requests): about 28,000 -> 13,000 (first two steps) -> 11,000 -> about 9,500 (51 x 180 + 12 x 30). The macOS E2E is shop 3.7 + album 2.7 min (run 40064478805), hence 15 -> 12.
- Order: (1) ci.yml: `plan` (OS and docs-only checks, merge-tree skip), `ci`, `timeout-minutes`; require `ci` in branch protection; remove the framework from the image workflows' `pull_request.paths` (48% on its own); (2) `script/ci-local`, the E2E shell moved into it, CONTRIBUTING.md, PR template, CLAUDE.md, pre-push hook; (3) E2E merge, Blog Dockerfile layers, the gate check; (4) propose dependency- or content-hash-based freshness to matz/spinel; (5) re-measure the table above after a month and add any macOS-only failure to `MACOS_PATHS`, recording it here.
- Consequences:
  - What CI no longer catches on the pull request: a macOS-only failure outside `MACOS_PATHS` (caught by the macOS run on main, or the daily one; covered by growing the list from each such failure and by `ci:macos`); an image broken by a framework change (caught on main after the merge; accepted because pull-request images are never pushed to anyone, a red main run is fixed in the next pull request, no pull-request image build failed in the 60 runs, and the usual breakage, gemspec vs cli-src, is already caught by `script/gate`; `ci:images` opts in). The full `spin test` still runs on every pull request that is not docs-only.
  - The merge-tree skip relies on the pull request head having passed CI, which is why `ci` must be the required check.
  - Testing moves to the developer machine and the Claude session; CI becomes the confirmation of what passed locally, and the round trips of reading a red CI and fixing it drop.
- Rejected:
  - Caching `build/test/` with `actions/cache` (key `hashFiles('cybertrain.rb','cybertrain/**','test/**','spin.toml')`, restore-keys, `git-restore-mtime`): any changed file rebuilds all 82 programs, so a pull request almost never hits; restoring another commit's `build/` with mtimes reset can make an old binary look newer than the changed source and "pass"; an exact-match key never hits a pull request that changes anything.
  - Merge queue (one run against the merged state): only for organization-owned repositories, and this one belongs to a personal account. Revisit if it moves to an organization.
  - A self-hosted runner: more upkeep, no gain while public-repository minutes are free.
  - Thinning the tests or not running `spin test` on pull requests: merging unaware of a failure costs more than the minutes.
  - Dropping macOS entirely: most cybertrain users develop on macOS (the OpenSSL note in CONTRIBUTING.md, the spike measurements); at least the daily run on main stays.
  - Publishing the playground image only on tags: `.devcontainer` uses `:latest`, so main keeps publishing it.
- Deferred: the 9-minute full compile itself, which needs a faster Spinel compile, fewer test binaries (bundling the 82 programs, a change to how tests are written) or dependency-based freshness in spin (a proposal to matz/spinel), all outside this design; whether to take the schedule variant for macOS; the `ci:images` label; `test/cli_new.rb` hanging partway through its first test block under `spin test` in the cloud session (4 of 4 runs; the same binary run directly from /tmp passes 23 tests, 154 assertions, and CI passes), to be investigated separately (cwd, or stdout under `xargs -P`).
- Implementation status (2026-11-11, the CI audit, issue #195): a first job `changes` now decides which jobs a run needs (#200), which is the docs-only half of `plan` above and not the rest of it: `on.paths` still starts the workflow, and when every changed file is one only the API reference reads (`docs/api/**`, `script/api-docs`, `.yardopts`) the jobs that compile are skipped and the new `api-docs` job, which runs on every CI run, is the only one left. The rule is `script/ci-changes` (`--self-test` checks it); the changed files come from git in a depth-2 checkout (the merge commit's first parent on a pull request, `github.event.before` on main), not from the API, and a run that cannot list them runs everything, as a release run does. Every job of CI, Gate and Pages now has `timeout-minutes` (#203), set from the runs read on 2026-11-11 and not the single 30 proposed above, which the macOS job has outgrown (177 test programs now, 82 then). In the last successful CI run on main (run 43010723711) the `framework-tests` shards took 10.5 to 17 minutes (limit 45), `test (ubuntu-26.04)` 23 (60) and `test (macos-latest)` 65, of which `spin test` was 47 (120); the eight successful runs on main before it took 21 to 42 minutes as a whole. `changes` (5), `api-docs` (10; its build was a 12-second step) and `setup-cybertrain` (30) are new and unmeasured. The last eight Gate runs took 20 to 27 seconds as a whole (10 for each job) and the last eight Pages runs 31 to 43 seconds, one of them 7 minutes (15). Gate also cancels the run for a pull request's previous commit, as CI does; its runs on main and in a release each have a concurrency group of their own, so none is cancelled or left pending. The OS choice, the merge-tree skip, the `ci` job to require and `script/ci-local` are still not implemented.
- Merge queue (2026-11-11, issue #209): not used, and nothing here asks for one. `main` has no rule that requires a queue (the audit found no branch protection at all), one maintainer merges with a merge commit, and the merge-tree skip above assumes that. Whether to use one is the repository owner's decision. Should it be turned on (Settings, Rules, the ruleset for `main`, "Require merge queue"), the queue waits for the required checks on a `merge_group` event, and a required workflow that lacks that trigger never reports, so the queue stalls. Gate and CI therefore carry `merge_group:` already; without a queue nothing sends the event. `merge_group` takes no `paths`, so every group starts CI, and `changes` compares the group with `github.event.merge_group.base_sha` to skip the jobs it does not need. To do when it is turned on, since none of it can be tried without a queue: give `merge_group:` to every other workflow made a required check (the image workflows start on their own `pull_request` paths only); merge one test pull request through the queue and check that its Gate and CI runs report under the check names the ruleset requires, that `changes` found its base (its log says "changed since"), and that neither run was cancelled; and decide the queue's merge method, since a squash or rebase queue ends the "merge commit whose tree equals its second parent's" test of the merge-tree skip.
- Implementation status (2026-11-05): the shards shipped, and none of the rest. This does not cut the minutes this section is about: it shortens the wait, not the total, and each shard pays for its own setup. On Ubuntu `spin test` moved out of the `test` job into a job `framework-tests`, four shards (`script/test-shard <index> <total>`, position in the sorted `test/*.rb` modulo the total), since each test program compiles on its own. `spin test` was 9 of the 21 minutes on Ubuntu and 6 of the 15 on macOS in the measurement above, so the end-to-end steps left in `test` still set the wait. The first run sharded both OSes: Ubuntu's last job ended after 7.9 minutes against 22.6 on main (one run each), but the five macOS jobs ran one after another and the whole run took 23.4 minutes, so macOS stays one job. The setup both jobs need moved into a composite action, `.github/actions/setup-spinel`. `test` keeps its name and holds the API reference, the end-to-end checks, examples/blog and, on macOS, `spin test`. The status below still holds for everything else.
- Implementation status (2026-10-28): none of it. ci.yml still runs the `[ubuntu-latest, macos-latest]` matrix on both `pull_request` and `push` with the docs-only rule as `paths`, has no `plan` or `ci` job and no `timeout-minutes`; the Playground and Blog image workflows still list the framework paths under `pull_request`; examples/blog/Dockerfile still copies all of `cybertrain/` before `cybertrain setup`; there is no `script/ci-local`, `.githooks/pre-push` (only pre-commit and commit-msg) or root `CLAUDE.md`, and the PR template still asks for `spin test`. Since the measurement, ci.yml gained a fourth E2E app (club, `generate authentication --invite-only`, D29), so the E2E merge above should be re-planned with it.

### D35. Several processes on one port with `SO_REUSEPORT`, not prefork (2026-11-05)

- Context: with `SPINEL_WORKERS=1` a server uses one CPU, and rule 22 keeps that the fast setting. Reads are what a second CPU can help. Measured on `examples/blog`'s `show` page (Linux arm64 in Docker on a Mac, wrk 32 connections, a process per CPU):

| Processes | `show` req/s | POST |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | about 9,100 | flat |
| 2 | about 16,000 | flat |
| 4 | about 27,600 | flat |

- Decision: `Server.new(..., reuse_port: false)` and `Config#reuse_port` (`CYBERTRAIN_REUSE_PORT`, default off). On, each process binds with `SO_REUSEPORT` and the Linux kernel splits connections between the listeners. Processes are independent: systemd starts them, nothing supervises them from inside (docs/deploy.md, "Running several processes").
  - Why not prefork: Spinel has no `fork` (a compiled `fork` raises `NameError`), so no parent can share a listener with its children, and `SO_REUSEPORT` needs no parent at all.
  - `TCPServer.new` has no option for it, so `bind_listener` builds a `Socket` (`SO_REUSEADDR`, `SO_REUSEPORT`, `bind`, `listen(1024)`) and hands it over with `TCPServer.for_fd`. The accepted sockets match a plain listener's: compared under Spinel, the same close-on-exec and non-blocking flags and `TCP_NODELAY`.
  - **One descriptor, two objects.** After `for_fd` the `Socket` and the `TCPServer` hold one number, each thinking it owns it. Measured under Spinel: closing the second closes whatever has reused the number, and collecting the `Socket` does the same (the test fails with `EPIPE` on a pipe that took the number; `EBADF` under CRuby). So the `Socket` has `autoclose = false`, stays in `@reuse_socket`, and the `TCPServer` closes, once, in `accept_loop`.
  - A host is resolved first, because the family must be known before `Socket.new`. An IPv6 literal binds as `AF_INET6`. A host that does not resolve or that this machine does not have raises `PortInUse` with `REUSE_PORT_HOST`, naming `reuse_port`, and `Application#serve` prints it as any other boot failure. That is also why there is no `Config#reuse_port_error`: only a bind can tell, and the plain path reports through the same exception.
  - **A name must have one address.** One with several raises `PortInUse` with `REUSE_PORT_NAME`, so `localhost` stops the boot where it has both families. Measured under CRuby and Spinel: `TCPServer.new("localhost", port)` binds `::1` only, and when `::1` is busy it binds `127.0.0.1` instead of raising. So no choice of address keeps a plain process out: it boots on the one left over, and clients of that family reach old code. Giving the plain path one address too would change the default bind of every application, and under Spinel `TCPServer.new` raises `ECONNREFUSED` for a busy address and a missing one alike, so that path could not tell when to move on.
  - Development ignores the setting with one warning: the dev server re-execs itself, and a stray second process would answer with stale code.
- Consequences:
  - A leftover process gets no bind error and keeps taking traffic with its old code. A plain listener and a `reuse_port` one still refuse each other in both orders (`PortInUse`, tested), so only processes that all asked for it share. That takes a single address, which is why a name with several stops the boot. Two plain processes on such a name still both boot, one per address, as they did before this setting.
  - The balancing is Linux's. macOS binds both and gives every connection to the last one bound (1000 of 1000 measured), so `test/server.rb` asserts the split only when `RUBY_PLATFORM` says Linux.
  - Everything kept in process memory is per process: `RateLimit` is N times looser with `store: :memory` (since D61 the default `store: :cache` is shared). `Scheduler`, the `Job` worker and `Background` run in every process that keeps `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER`, so all but one set it to 0. The `--invite-only` setup token is per process too, so a first run happens on one process.
  - POST does not scale: SQLite has one writer.
  - Linux drops the connections still in the accept queue of a listener that closes, as with any restart; not measured here.
- Rejected:
  - `SPINEL_WORKERS` above 1: slower than one (rule 22), and a process is the unit that scales here.
  - A proxy in front balancing several ports: more moving parts in the deploy for what the kernel does.
- Verification: `test/server.rb` (two servers on one port both answer, both take connections on Linux, one stopping leaves the other; stop closes the descriptor once and the port binds again; plain and `reuse_port` refuse each other; a name with several addresses, an IPv6 literal, a host that does not resolve, an address not on this machine), `test/application.rb` (the environment variable, production and test pass it on, development warns once and ignores it). Two processes through wrk on `/articles/1`, one CPU each, alternating, in the same container with other benchmarks running: 1.49, 1.56, 1.73, 1.75, 1.86, 1.94 times one process (median of each 10,055 and 16,702 req/s, 1.66), with both processes taking the same CPU time in every round.

### D36. Streaming responses and the stream endpoint: Server-Sent Events and WebSocket for browsers (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: fin_dash pushes prices, order books and alerts to open pages (Action Cable and Turbo Streams in Rails). CyberTrain had no way to keep a response open: a `Response` was one String written once (D5), and the WebSocket code was a client only (D33). [The phase 1 design](superpowers/specs/2026-11-05-phase1-realtime-views-processes-design.md) section 2 has the full specification.
- Decision, streaming responses: a `Response` may carry a `BodyWriter` instead of a body (D5's revision). `Controller#stream(content_type: "text/event-stream", status:) { |out| ... }` is Rails' `ActionController::Live` without the module: the block runs on the connection's thread after the head is sent and receives a `Sink` (`write`, `closed?`, `wait_closed(seconds)`, `socket`). `Cybertrain::SSE.event(data, event: "", id: "", retry_ms: 0)` and `SSE.comment(text)` format events (`retry` is a Ruby keyword, so the field is `retry_ms`; a CR or LF in the data starts a new `data:` line, so a payload cannot end the event early). `stream` marks the response performed, as `render` does.
- Decision, the endpoint: one middleware, `Stream::Endpoint`, mounted by `Application#build_stack` at `Config#stream_path` (default `/cybertrain/stream`; `""` turns the feature off), inside the request logger and before the health check, the static files, the session, CSRF and the router (it sets no cookie). It answers `GET` and `HEAD` only (405 otherwise):
  - without `Upgrade`: Server-Sent Events, with `Cache-Control: no-store` and `X-Accel-Buffering: no`, each message as an event with `id:`, a `: ping` comment after `stream_ping_interval` (15) seconds of silence;
  - with `Upgrade: websocket`: the RFC 6455 handshake (version 13, a 16-byte key, `Connection: upgrade`; 400 otherwise), then a reader thread for the client's frames and the connection thread for every write: a ping after the same silence, pongs, the close handshake. Frames to the client are unmasked (`Frame.encode(op, payload, "")`, frame.rb unchanged); extensions and subprotocols are not negotiated (`permessage-deflate` is answered without it, as RFC 6455 allows).
- Two modes on both transports: `mode=raw` (the default) sends the payload as it is, with no `event:` field, so Turbo's `<turbo-stream-source>` (over `EventSource` or `WebSocket`) works unchanged; `mode=json` sends `{"stream":...,"id":...,"data":...}` (over SSE also `event: <stream name>`, so `addEventListener(name, ...)` dispatches by stream), and over WebSocket the client may send `{"subscribe":"<token>"}` / `{"unsubscribe":"<token>"}`, answered `{"subscribed":[names]}`, `{"unsubscribed":[names]}` or `{"error":"bad token"}` (also `"bad message"`, `"too many streams"`). That is the path for hand-written JavaScript.
- Authorisation is the signature (Turbo's model). `s=<token>` (repeatable) is `Stream.sign(names)`: base64url of the names joined by `,`, `--`, and the hex HMAC-SHA256 of that text keyed with a key derived for streams alone, the hex digest `hex(HMAC-SHA256(secret_key_base, "cybertrain/stream"))` used as the key's bytes (`Stream.signing_key`, as Rails' `turbo/signed_stream_verifier_key`; the hex text, not the raw digest, is the key): a session cookie has the same `<base64>--<hex HMAC>` layout keyed with the secret itself, so without the separation a token for the names `{"user_id":"1"` and `"x":"y"}` would load as a session. A page that could render the token had the right to read its streams; a per-user stream is `Stream.name_for(current_user)` rendered only for that user. The endpoint reads neither the session nor the database to decide. A bad or missing token is a 403 over SSE and, over WebSocket, a 101 followed by close 1008 (what a browser's `WebSocket` can report). Tokens do not expire; rotating the secret invalidates all of them. Names are 1 to 200 bytes of UTF-8 with no control character and no comma, at most 16 per token and 64 per connection.
- Limits: `stream_max_connections` (1000) open streams per process, checked before the head and again under the hub's lock (503 over SSE, close 1013 over WebSocket); `stream_max_message` (65536) bytes per client frame (close 1009); a reader queues at most 64 control messages; a mode=json WebSocket that listens to no stream for one ping interval is closed with 1008, so pings alone cannot hold a slot.
- The endpoint answers 503 (`stream poller is not running`) until the process's stream poller (D37) runs: `Application#serve` starts the poller before the listener binds, so in a served process the 503 is never seen; a stack built without `serve` (a test) must start it.
- Reason: `<turbo-stream-source>` opens an `EventSource` for an `http(s)` URL and a `WebSocket` for a `ws(s)` one, so one endpoint with both serves Turbo either way. SSE passes every proxy, reconnects by itself and sends `Last-Event-ID`, which makes replay (D37) free; WebSocket is what hand-written dashboards use and what fin_dash's channels are. The signature keeps a connect free of session and database work and works the same in every process.
- Rejected: the Action Cable protocol (channel classes with server-side `subscribed` callbacks need a computed dispatch per channel and a JavaScript client to ship; the browser's own `EventSource` and `WebSocket` are documented instead); subscriptions checked against the session (Turbo signs names instead, and a per-connection session or database read would sit on every reconnect); a second port or process for streams; long polling; token expiry (Turbo has none; a short expiry breaks long-open pages); `permessage-deflate`.
- Judgement calls (spec section 13, recorded in the code too): a write to a client times out after `stream_ping_interval` (`write_nonblock` + `wait_writable`; `Server`'s `stream_write_timeout`) and `SO_KEEPALIVE` is set best effort, so a vanished client is noticed after a FIN or RST or a timed-out write, not within one ping interval of silently vanishing; the WebSocket reader's 64-message cap and the 1008 for a connection with no streams; the `s` token is written `[FILTERED]` in the framework's request log on the stream path (a proxy's access log still sees it, and the guide says so); the `Accept` header is not checked, so any `GET` without `Upgrade` is SSE.
- Consequences: a stream holds its connection's green thread for its life and is never kept alive afterwards; shutdown shuts the read side of every streaming connection, so streams end with the drain (WebSocket clients get close 1000). A reverse proxy must not buffer the SSE response (the `X-Accel-Buffering` header covers nginx) and must pass `Upgrade` for WebSocket. `Test::Client#get_stream(path, events:, timeout:, headers:)` runs a stream's writer in memory until `events` events.
- Configuration: `stream_path` (`CYBERTRAIN_STREAM_PATH`), `stream_ping_interval`, `stream_max_connections` and `stream_max_message`, checked at boot by `Config#stream_error` (the path must start with `/` and differ from `health_path`; each number has a floor, 125 for the message size).
- Verification: `test/response_stream.rb`, `test/sse.rb`, `test/stream_endpoint.rb` and `test/websocket_server.rb` cover it.

### D37. The stream pub/sub: a SQLite table, one poller per web process, and Turbo Stream broadcasts (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: a broadcast can come from any process (an action, a job in a `worker`, a feed callback in `feeds`, a `Main.command`), and every web process must deliver it to the browsers it holds. The one thing all of them share is the SQLite file.
- Decision: `Stream.broadcast(name, payload)` inserts a row into `cybertrain_messages (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT, stream, payload, created_at)` with an index on `(stream, id)`, created by `Stream.install!` (`CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, once per connected database, as `Job.install!`), and returns its id. Inside a `DB.transaction` the message is sent only if the transaction commits.
- Every broadcast goes through the table, the web process's own included: two web processes must both deliver, and the table gives one order and one id space, which `Last-Event-ID` needs. Latency is bounded by the poll interval.
- Names: `Stream.name_for(record)` is `"articles:12"` (table name and id; an unsaved record raises), and a String passes through. `Stream.sign(names)` / `Stream.verify(token)` are the token's two ends (D36); `Stream.prune(before)` deletes older rows.
- The poller (`Stream::Poller`): one thread per `server` process (also with `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0`: it delivers to that process's browsers), started by `Application#serve` **before the listener binds**, never lazily. Its cursor `last_id` starts at the current `MAX(id)` (0 on an empty table). Every `stream_poll_ms` (100, at least 10) it reads `SELECT MAX(id)` (one B-tree descent on the primary key, no dedicated connection) and, when that passed `last_id`, the new rows, which it hands to the hub. `last_id` advances to a row's id only after the hub delivered it, under the hub's lock. Every minute it deletes the rows older than `stream_retention` (60 s): the poller is the one owner of pruning.
- The hub (`Stream::Hub`): `subscribe(names)` returns a `Subscriber` with a `Queue` of messages (`Queue#pop(timeout:)` works under Spinel, NOTES rule 85) and a **boundary**, set to the poller's `last_id` under the hub's lock; `deliver` pushes onto every matching queue and skips a message with `id <= boundary`. So a fresh subscription receives only rows the poller had not yet delivered when it subscribed (one poll interval of slack, which no client can observe), never the backlog; the cutoff is per subscriber, and a connect runs no SQL. A queue longer than `stream_max_backlog` (1000) drops the subscriber: the stream ends and the client reconnects, as Action Cable does with a client that cannot keep up.
- Replay: `Last-Event-ID` (SSE; it wins over the query) or `?after=<id>` replays the rows of the subscribed streams with a larger id that are still in the table, then goes live, with no row lost or doubled: the connection subscribes first (its queue starts filling), replays, sets the boundary to the largest replayed id (or to the replay's start when nothing came back) and drains the queue past it. The replay is paged: 200 rows a chunk (`Stream.replay`, filtered by stream in SQL on the index), each chunk written before the next is read, so a long backlog never sits in memory, and the boundary only passes rows that were sent. A start above the head (an id from another database, or one a client made up with a valid token) is clamped to the subscription's cursor, so it cannot starve itself of every later row.
- Turbo: `Cybertrain::Turbo.replace / update / append / prepend / before / after(target, html)`, `remove(target)` and `refresh` build `<turbo-stream action="..." target="..."><template>...</template></turbo-stream>` (a target is a DOM id or a record; the HTML is escaped unless it is a `SafeString`). `Stream.broadcast_replace_to(stream, target:, html: | partial: with locals: | component:)` and its `update`, `append`, `prepend`, `before`, `after`, `remove` and `refresh` siblings render (through `Views.render_partial` / `render_component`, D38), wrap and broadcast, as turbo-rails' `broadcast_*_to`. The template side is `turbo_stream_from(*streamables, ws: false)` (one `<turbo-stream-source>` with one token for all the names), `signed_stream_name` and `stream_source_path(..., mode: "json")`.
- Configuration: `stream_poll_ms` (100, at least 10), `stream_retention` (60 s) and `stream_max_backlog` (1000), checked by `Config#stream_error` at boot.
- Spinel: the poller thread starts through a method whose parameters the block captures (NOTES rule 21); the hub's subscriber lists are seeded with their element type (rule 9); a generation number keeps a poller loop left from before a `stop!` from running beside a new one; names are compared with a `==` loop rather than `include?` (rule 29).
- Reason: SQLite is the only medium every role shares without a new dependency; 100 ms of latency is invisible on a dashboard and costs one primary-key descent per poll per process. A per-subscriber boundary taken from the poller's cursor gives the "no backlog, nothing missed" rule without a query per connection.
- Where the poller runs: every `server` process, and only there (`worker`, `feeds`, `console` and `Test::Client` run none). A broadcast from any role is a row; the web processes pick it up.
- Rejected: an in-process hub alone (a broadcast from a job in a `worker` would reach no page); Redis or a LISTEN/NOTIFY database (a dependency CyberTrain does not have; SQLite only, D10); `PRAGMA data_version` as the change check (its value is per connection, and the pool hands out any connection); a `MAX(id)` query per connection for its cutoff (costs SQL on every reconnect for a slack no client sees); one process-wide cutoff (a subscription taken while a row is in flight would miss it); starting the poller lazily on the first subscription (a subscription before the cursor existed could miss or double rows; hence the eager start and the 503); an unpaged replay (a retention's worth of rows in memory per reconnect).
- Judgement calls (spec section 13): the paged replay and its boundary rule; the clamp of a start above the head; pruning owned by the poller alone (a deployment with only `worker` processes has no readers and is not a target, and nothing prunes there).
- Consequences: live delivery reaches the subscribers present when a row is polled; a client away longer than `stream_retention` misses what was pruned and should reload (`broadcast_refresh_to` is the Turbo way to ask). Each broadcast is a write and takes SQLite's write lock, so a feed of hundreds of messages a second should batch. Like `cybertrain_jobs` and `cybertrain_leases`, the table is left out of the `db/schema.rb` dump (`SchemaDumper.table_names`), so the generator never makes a model of it; a record's target id comes from `Html.dom_id`, the same id `dom_id` prints in a template.
- Verification: `test/stream.rb` (the table, tokens, the hub, the boundary, the poller, paged replay and the clamp, pruning) and `test/turbo.rb` (the builders and `broadcast_*_to`); `test/stream_endpoint.rb` drives replay and live delivery end to end.

### D38. Application helpers and view components: a lexical scan into `case` tables, and rendering outside a request (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: Rails mixes the modules under `app/helpers` into every view and ViewComponent renders `app/components`; fin_dash uses both. CyberTrain's templates could call only the built-in helpers (D9), and a template could be rendered only inside a request, so a broadcast (D37) or a mail body (D27) had no way to reuse a partial.
- Decision, helpers: `app/helpers/**/*.rb` holds plain modules (`module PricesHelper; def price(value, unit: "USD", precision: 2) ... end; end`). `Gen::HelperScan` reads them as text, as `ControllerScan` does (D4): the `module` lines and the public instance `def`s, skipping `def self.`, setters, everything after `private` / `protected` up to `public`, the bodies of defs and of nested classes. Each parameter list is split into required and optional positionals, required and optional keywords and `&block`. `gen/helpers.rb` defines `ApplicationHelpers < Cybertrain::Template::Helpers`, includes `Gen::UrlHelpers` and every module, and overrides `app_helper_call(name, args, kwargs, block, interp, env)` with a `case` on the name: an arity check, then **one arm per subset of the optional parameters present**, so an omitted keyword gets the method's own default as in Ruby. `Views.helpers_builder` (set by `gen/helpers.rb`) is how controllers and `Views.render_partial` build the helpers; the base `app_helper_call` raises `undefined helper`.
- Errors the generator reports, naming the file and line: `*rest`, `**opts`, `...` and anonymous parameters ("helper parameters must be named; templates pass values by position or keyword"); a required parameter after an optional one; more than three optional parameters (eight arms: split the helper); the same name in two modules (Rails' silent override is a classic bug); and a **reserved name**: any method of `Template::Helpers` or its helper base, public or private, any built-in helper name, `yield`, or a name ending in `_path` / `_url` (the route helpers answer those). `HelperScan::RESERVED_HELPER_NAMES` holds the list, and `script/check-reserved-names` keeps it complete against helpers.rb and interpreter.rb. A private `def` is checked too: `include` would replace the framework's method of that name all the same, and NOTES rule 34 forbids one name with two return types.
- Dispatch order for a receiverless name: the built-in helpers, then (inside a component's template) the component's view methods, then `*_path` / `*_url` (route helpers; `*_url` needs a request), then `app_helper_call`, whose base raises `undefined helper 'x'`. Dispatch goes through the base class, as `Middleware` and `Background::Service` do.
- Helpers receive the template's polymorphic values and narrow them with `Cast`; a `&block` parameter receives the block's captured output as a `SafeString`. Ruby-side helpers call the framework through Ruby-shaped APIs: `Html.tag`, `Html.escape`, `Html.safe_join`, `Html.class_names`, `Html.dom_id`, `SafeString.of`, the URL helpers, `link_to_path(text, path, attrs = {})` and `button_to_path(text, path, attrs = {})`.
- Decision, components: `class CardComponent < Cybertrain::Component` with keyword arguments in `initialize`, and `card_component.html.erb` beside it. `Gen::ComponentScan` picks up the class line, the `@x =` assignments (the template's locals, as a controller's ivars), the arity-0 public `def`s (callable by name) and the `initialize` keywords (keywords only; at most three optional). A `content` keyword or `@content` ivar is a scan error naming the file and line (rendering sets `@content` to the block's output and would overwrite it silently; ViewComponent raises `ReservedParameterError` for the same name; added in the phase 1 review). `gen/components.rb` writes per class `view_assigns`, `call_view_method` / `view_method?`, `component_name` and `component_template`, and `Gen::Components.build(name, kwargs)`, the `case` from a template's component name to the class's `new` (subset arms again), installed as `Views.component_builder`. A template renders one with `<%= render component: "card", title: "x" %>`, with `do ... end` for its `content`; a namespaced class is `"admin/user_row"`. The template renders without a layout, counts against `max_render_depth`, and answers the component's view methods first for a receiverless name; a partial rendered from it does not see them.
- Rendering outside a request: `Views.render_partial(name, locals)` and `Views.render_component(component, content = "")` build the application's helpers with no controller. Path helpers work (`Views.url_resolver` is process-wide); the request-bound helpers (`csrf_token`, `csrf_meta_tags`, `params`, `flash`, `request_path`, `*_url`, `turbo_stream_from ws: true`) raise `Views::OutsideRequest, "csrf_token needs a request"`. Strict locals are checked as in a request. `Test.render_partial` and `Test.render_component` wrap them for tests.
- Templates call an application helper as they call a built-in one: `<%= price @item.cost, unit: "EUR" %>`, and with a block, `<%= panel "Title" do %>...<% end %>`.
- Components may render partials and other components; a component's own `render component:` nests like a partial's `render`.
- Generators: `cybertrain generate helper NAME` (an empty module and a test) and `cybertrain generate component NAME [attr[:type]...]` (the class, its template and a test that uses `Test.render_component`).
- Reason: Rails' layout and vocabulary, with the dispatch Spinel allows: there is no `send` or `const_get`, so every name-to-method step is a generated `case` on a literal (D8, D9). The subset arms keep a helper a plain Ruby method with Ruby's default semantics, callable from Ruby and from templates alike.
- Rejected: a computed `send` over the helper names (the 128-literal limit, NOTES rule 2); handing every helper one `kwargs` Hash (helpers would stop being ordinary methods and lose their defaults); splats in helper signatures (a generated arm has to name every parameter); letting an application helper override a framework one; naming components by constant in templates (constants stay out of templates; a snake-case name, as for partials); `component.render_in(view)` (there is no view object to render in).
- Judgement calls (spec section 13): `Html.attributes` is called `Html.tag_attributes` (`Model#attributes` returns a Hash, NOTES rule 34); `dom_id`'s prefix is positional, as in Rails; `button_to_path` takes `method:` inside `attrs`; `OutsideRequest` is raised only by helpers built through `render_partial` / `render_component`, while a bare `Template::Helpers.new(nil)` stays lenient (empty values) for unit tests; `form_with` and `button_to` render outside a request without the authenticity token (a Turbo page submits it from `csrf_meta_tags`); a pager in a broadcast partial carries only its path's own query string; the scan skips def bodies, setters and singleton methods, and a `def` line inside a heredoc is an accepted limit of a lexical scan.
- Consequences: adding a helper or a component is a Ruby change (`spin run gen` and a rebuild), unlike a view edit, and the development server does both. A component's template, like a view, is read from disk in development and embedded by `cybertrain build` (`ViewsEmitter` embeds `app/components/**/*.erb` under `__components/`, `Template::Engine::COMPONENTS_PREFIX`). The prefix was `components/` until the phase 1 review: a view of a `ComponentsController` (`app/views/components/...`) was then read from `app/components`, and its embedded key collided with a component template's. No view path starts with `__components/` unless an application makes `app/views/__components/`, which is reserved.
- Verification: `test/gen_helpers.rb`, `test/app_helpers.rb`, `test/gen_components.rb`, `test/component_render.rb`, `test/views_detached.rb`, `test/html_tag.rb`, `test/template_case.rb`, `test/template_blocks.rb`, `test/template_tag.rb`, `test/cli_generate_helper.rb` and `test/cli_generate_component.rb` cover it.

### D39. Process roles and the scheduler lease: `server`, `worker`, `feeds` (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: one process did everything (HTTP, the scheduler, jobs, feeds), and `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0` was the only way to run several against one database (D28). fin_dash runs a web role, a jobs role and one container per feed, as Rails does with Puma, `bin/jobs` and separate processes.
- Decision: `Main.run` gains two subcommands beside `server`:
  - `./<app> server [PORT]`, unchanged: HTTP, the stream poller (D37), and, unless `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0`, the scheduler, the job threads and the feeds. One process doing everything stays the default, as `rails server` with Solid Queue in Puma.
  - `./<app> worker`: the scheduler (lease-gated), the job threads, heartbeats and recovery (D28); no port. Several may share a database.
  - `./<app> feeds [NAME...]`: the WebSocket subscriptions (D33), all or the named ones; no port, no jobs, no scheduler. An unknown name is a boot error listing the registered ones; with none registered it says so and exits 0.
  - `Application#run_worker` / `#run_feeds` boot (config checks, database), print a banner (`=> worker: 3 job threads, 4 scheduled tasks`), trap TERM and INT (Ctrl-C drains as TERM does; INT added in the phase 1 review), start, wait for the trap, and stop in `serve`'s order. `Config#worker` is forced on in both roles: a `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0` meant for the web role in a shared environment must not turn off the process that is there to do the work. `Main.worker?(argv)` and `Main.feeds?(argv)` join `Main.server?(argv)` for `bin/<app>.rb`.
- The lease: `cybertrain_leases (name TEXT PRIMARY KEY, holder TEXT NOT NULL, expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL)` and `Lease.acquire(name, holder, now, ttl:)`, one statement (`INSERT ... ON CONFLICT(name) DO UPDATE ... WHERE holder = excluded.holder OR expires_at < ?`, success when `changes == 1`) that takes a free lease, renews one's own and takes over an expired one; `Lease.release`. Every scheduler tick acquires `"scheduler"` for `scheduler_lease_ttl` (30 s, at least twice `scheduler_interval`); only the holder runs due tasks, and it renews the lease before each task; the others keep ticking and take over once it expires. `Scheduler.leader?` is readable; `stop!` releases the lease at once. Recovery and heartbeats run in every process that runs jobs, leader or not (idempotent, D28). The table is created on first use and left out of `db/schema.rb`, as `cybertrain_jobs`.
- `Background::Service#service_start_only(config, names)` (the default starts the service only when `names` is empty) and `service_feed_names`; `WebSocket.start!(config, only: names)`.
- Configuration: `scheduler_lease_ttl` (30 s), checked at boot with the job settings (`Config#scheduler_error`: each at least 1, `job_heartbeat_stale` above `job_heartbeat_interval`, the lease at least twice `scheduler_interval`, so a leader that ticks on time never lets it lapse).
- Health: `GET /up` keeps reporting the scheduler's `last_tick` (a non-leader still ticks), so a worker that stopped ticking is `worker stale` there whatever the lease says.
- Upgrading a running deployment: the NULL-heartbeat recovery rule (D28) holds only once no pre-heartbeat worker runs (a legacy worker still executing a `running` row writes no heartbeat, and a new worker would requeue its job), so every old process is stopped before the first new one starts. One worker per database was the old rule, so this is the existing deploy order. docs/deploy.md and docs/deploy.en.md say so in "Splitting the roles"; `cybertrain new` writes `web`, `worker` (`cmd: ./<app> worker`) and a commented `feeds` role into `config/deploy.yml`, one image for all.
- Reason: the roles a Rails deployment has, with the binary's own subcommands; the lease makes "several workers" safe for recurring tasks without a coordinator.
- Rejected: a supervisor inside the binary that forks the roles (Spinel's prefork is unfinished, and systemd and Kamal already supervise); a `--role` flag instead of subcommands (Rails and Solid Queue use commands, and `Main.command` already reads the first word); a lock file (it does not span hosts or containers sharing a volume over a network); a lease per task (one lease is enough while tasks stay short, and a long one should `Job.enqueue`).
- Judgement calls (spec section 13): a non-leader tick advances the timetable too, so after a leader change a task may run again sooner than its interval, rather than every overdue task at once; the lease is re-acquired before each task, so a leader that lost it mid-tick stops; `service_start_only` starts nothing when feed names are given; the TERM trap also stops claiming (`Job.request_stop`), so the shutdown stays inside systemd's 15 s.
- Consequences: a `worker` serves no HTTP and runs no stream poller; a broadcast from it is delivered by the `server` processes. A `server` with `CYBERTRAIN_WORKER=0` still runs the poller. `test/lease.rb`, `test/main_roles.rb` and `test/job_threads.rb` cover the lease and the roles; `migrate`, `db`, `Main.command`, `console` and `Test::Client` start nothing, as before.

### D40. BLOB columns: `t.binary`, `DB::Blob`, and a byte-exact read (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: fin_dash stores compressed snapshots and images in the database. Every String was bound with `sqlite3_bind_text` and read with `sqlite3_column_text`, and both stop at the first NUL, which is why D16 kept upload bytes out of SQLite.
- Decision: `t.binary :body` in the schema DSL and `add_column :t, :c, :binary` in a migration give a `BLOB` column (emitted unquoted, like the other types); the dumper writes a `BLOB` column back as `t.binary`.
- The generated attribute is a `String` (`String | nil` for a nullable column) holding the raw bytes; a template or the console sees it as a String too.
- Writing: the generated `to_row` wraps each binary column's value in `Cybertrain::DB::Blob.new(bytes)` (`Blob.wrap` keeps a nil as NULL), and `Connection#bind` sends a `Blob` with `sqlite3_bind_blob(stmt, i, bytes, bytesize, SQLITE_TRANSIENT)`. Every other String still binds as TEXT, so no existing column changes. A raw query binds the same way: `c.execute("INSERT INTO images (data) VALUES (?)", [Cybertrain::DB::Blob.new(bytes)])`; `Cast.binds` accepts a `Blob`.
- Reading: `read_row` sees `SQLITE_BLOB` and calls `cybertrain_sqlite_column_blob`, a `:binstr` FFI function over `sqlite3_column_blob` and `sqlite3_column_bytes` (the pattern of `GzipFFI.cybertrain_bytes_at`), which returns a String of exactly the stored bytes, NULs included. Raw SQL callers get the same String; `Cast.binary(value)` narrows a cell or attribute to a String (`""` for nil, a `Blob`'s bytes).
- The console prints a binary value as `<N bytes>` (D41).
- Spinel: the bytes are copied with `<<` and sliced with `byteslice` (NOTES rule 60), never interpolated; the FFI return is `:binstr`, which carries a length, where `:str` would stop at a NUL.
- Upgrade: nothing changes for an existing database; a `BLOB` column that an older dump wrote as something else is dumped as `t.binary` from now on.
- Templates: a binary attribute is a String like any other, so `<%= @image.data.size %>` works; printing it would print the bytes (escaped), which is rarely wanted.
- What it is not: no streaming reads (`sqlite3_blob_open`), no size limit of its own beyond SQLite's (1,000,000,000 bytes by default), no Active Storage-style attachment over it.
- Reason: the Rails vocabulary (`t.binary`, a String attribute), and a wrapper only at the bind, where SQLite has to be told TEXT from BLOB; a String's encoding cannot say it under Spinel, and keying on encoding would turn existing UTF-8 text with a stray byte into BLOBs.
- Rejected: a separate `Bytes` value class for attributes (every caller would convert; Rails hands out a String); base64 in a TEXT column (a third more storage, and every reader decodes); binding every non-UTF-8 String as a BLOB (silently changes the column type of existing data).
- Judgement call (spec section 13): a `:str` FFI argument keeps embedded NULs when the callee is given an explicit length (NOTES rule 60), so `sqlite3_bind_blob` takes the bytes through `:str` with `bytesize`; a TEXT bind still passes -1 and stops at a NUL, as before.
- Consequences: a model can hold small binary values (thumbnails, compressed blobs, keys); large files still belong in `BlobStore` (D16), whose reasons for disk storage (backup size, WAL churn, whole-value reads) stand.
- Verification: `test/db_blob.rb` round-trips 0, 1 and 70,000 bytes with NULs and bytes above 127, through a model and through raw SQL, and checks the dumper.

### D41. The console and dbconsole: an expression console over the generated models (2026-11-05, phase 1)

- Context: `rails console` is how a Rails developer looks at data and fixes a record; Spinel has no `eval`, so it was listed under section 8 as left out, and the tutorial told readers there was no console.
- Decision, `./<app> console`: a read-eval-print loop over the **template expression language** (D9's grammar, with no blocks), evaluated by the same interpreter with `Console::Helpers`. Two console-only additions: the parser's `constants:` option turns `Article` into a constant node, which `Console::Helpers#constant(name)` resolves through the tables `gen/console.rb` sets (`Console.scope_builder`, `records_builder`, `create_builder`, `model_names`: a `case` per model, so the console file compiles without them and any name is `UnknownModel` until they are set); and the commands `sql "SELECT ..." [binds]` (rows as a table), `tables`, `help`, `exit` / `quit` / end of input.
- On a relation (the base class, so one arm serves every model): `where`, `where_sql`, `where_like`, `order`, `order_sql`, `limit`, `offset`, `page`, `first`, `last`, `find`, `find_by`, `to_a`, `count`, `exists?`, `pluck_str` / `pluck_int` / `pluck_float`, `sum_int` / `sum_float`, `count_by`, `delete_all`, `to_sql`, `create`. On a record: its attributes and view methods (as in templates), `update`, `save`, `destroy`, `reload`, `valid?`, `errors`, `attributes`. `x = expr` keeps a value for later lines and `_` is the last one.
- Output (`Console::Printer`): a record as `#<Article id: 1, title: "x", ...>` with every column, each value `inspect`-style and cut at 80 bytes with `…`; an Array one element per line with a count; a relation as its SQL and count; a binary value as `<N bytes>`; scalars as `inspect`. A line that does not parse prints its `SyntaxError`, one that raises prints `Class: message`, and the loop goes on; names a failed line bound are dropped. `CYBERTRAIN_ENV=production` prints a warning banner first and otherwise behaves the same, as Rails does.
- Line reading is `gets` on STDIN, one statement per line; a line starting with `#` and an empty line are skipped. The value of an assignment is printed as well.
- Errors keep their class: a method that raised inside an expression is reported with its own class and message (`Cybertrain::RecordNotFound: Couldn't find Article with id=9`), not the interpreter's wrapper.
- Decision, `./<app> dbconsole`: `Process.spawn("sqlite3", database_path)` and `Process.wait` (NOTES rule 75), the exit status passed through; a missing binary prints "sqlite3 not found: install the sqlite3 CLI". A path starting with `-` is given as `./-...`.
- Not there: blocks, method definitions, `reload!`, `app.get`, and anything else that is not an expression over the models; an application command (`Main.command`, D18) is the place for a script.
- Both are `Main` built-ins dispatched before `server?`: they connect a pool of one and start no scheduler, job thread or feed.
- Development and production run the same code; only the banner differs.
- Reason: most of what a developer does in `rails console` is a query, a record update or a look at a row, and the template language already evaluates exactly those expressions safely over the generated models; reusing the interpreter costs a parser option and a table per model. `dbconsole` covers everything else SQL can do.
- Rejected: embedding a Ruby interpreter (mruby or CRuby in the binary: a second runtime, against principle 5); compiling each line (a compile per line takes seconds and the result could not see the process's state); readline (a C dependency for line editing; `gets` is enough, and `rlwrap ./app console` adds editing).
- Judgement calls (spec section 13): `Printer` methods carry `_text` suffixes (a `format` or `inspect` shared with other classes would break whole-program inference, NOTES rules 10 / 34); the prompt is flushed before every `gets`, since under Spinel `gets` does not flush a pending partial line on STDOUT (NOTES rule 83); `dbconsole` traps INT in the parent, because Ctrl-C at the `sqlite3` prompt reaches the whole foreground group and the parent would die and leave `sqlite3` reading the terminal; rescued classes are spelt from the top (`Cybertrain::Template::SyntaxError`, NOTES rule 81).
- Consequences: the tutorial's "no console" callout and the guides change with this. In development the console is `cybertrain spin run <name> -- console` (the binary is compiled for each Ruby change, as for the server). Writes from the console are real; there is no sandbox mode (Rails' `--sandbox` would need a transaction held open across lines, which the pool of one could do, and is left for later).
- Verification: `test/console.rb`, `test/gen_console.rb` and `test/main_console.rb` cover it.

### D42. Column types and casting: decimal as Float, json as Hash, Rails' datetime format with `db normalize`, Date, and time parsing on `Time` (2026-11-07, phase 2)

- Context: fin_dash's schema has `decimal(18,8)` prices, `json` metadata, `datetime(6)` and `date` columns, all written by Rails 8.1.
